    - **Direct paths from B to M**:
      - B -> E -> J -> M
      - B -> E -> P -> M
      - B -> C -> H -> I -> P -> J -> M
      - B -> C -> H -> G -> K -> X -> D -> J -> M
3. **Find the minimum cut set**: To disconnect B and M, we need to remove nodes that are crucial in these paths. We need to ensure that there is no remaining path between B and M.
    - Removing **E** will cut off the direct routes B -> E -> J -> M and B -> E -> P -> M.
    - Removing **J** will cut off the paths involving J, including B -> E -> P -> M and B -> C -> H -> I -> P -> J -> M.
    - Removing **C** will cut off the paths involving C, including B -> C -> H -> I -> P -> J -> M and B -> C -> H -> G -> K -> X -> D -> J -> M.
    - Removing **H** will cut off the paths involving H, including B -> C -> H -> I -> P -> J -> M and B -> C -> H -> G -> K -> X -> D -> J -> M.
4. **Verify the minimum cut set**:
    - Removing both E and J will ensure that all paths from B to M are disconnected.
    - Checking other combinations, such as removing C and H, will also disconnect B and M, but removing E and J is the minimal set required to ensure no path exists between B and M.

- **Level 1:** L is connected to M, F, and G.
  - **Verify connections:**
    - Is node L directly connected to node M? Yes.
    - Is node L directly connected to node F? Yes.
    - Is node L directly connected to node G? Yes.

- **Level 2:** From each of L's neighbors, I explore further.
  - From M:
    - M is not directly connected to H.
  - From F:
    - F is connected to X, G, and K.
      - **Verify connections:**
        - Is node F directly connected to node X? Yes.
        - Is node F directly connected to node G? Yes.
        - Is node F directly connected to node K? Yes.
      - Since F is connected to G and G is also a direct neighbor of L, we skip the redundant path.
  - From G:
    - G is connected to X and H.
      - **Verify connections:**
        - Is node G directly connected to node X? Yes.
        - Is node G directly connected to node H? Yes.
      - G to H forms a path of length 2.

- **Verify shortest path:**
  - Path L → G → H has length 2.
  - Check for shorter paths:
    - L to M (Level 1) → (No direct path to H).
    - L to F (Level 1) → F to X (Level 2), no further paths to H.
  - No shorter path exists.

Final Answer: Length: 2, Path: [L, G, H]

--- Model Response ---
To solve this problem, we need to determine the minimum number of nodes that must be removed to completely disconnect the two black nodes, E and I, while ensuring that no paths exist between them.

### Step-by-step reasoning:

1. **Identify all paths between E and I:**
   - Path 1: E → H → B → C → I
   - Path 2: E → H → B → N → O → I
   - Path 3: E → M → L → J → P → I
   - Path 4: E → H → B → N → O → P → I
   - Path 5: E → M → L → J → D → O → P → I

2. **Group paths into disjoint sets:**
   - Paths that pass through node H: Path 1, Path 2, Path 4
   - Paths that pass through node M: Path 3, Path 5
   - The fourth path (E → H → B → N → O → P → I) essentially combines paths through H and M.

3. **Determine critical nodes:**
   - Removing node H will break all paths that go through H (Paths 1, 2, 4).
   - Removing node M will break all paths that go through M (Paths 3, 5).
   - Since both nodes H and M are critical and removing either alone won't suffice as there are still alternate paths, both need to be removed.

4. **Verify the minimal cut set:**
   - Removing either H or M alone does not disconnect E and I, as there are alternate paths.
   - Removing both H and M ensures that all possible paths between E and I are severed.

### Conclusion:
The minimum number of nodes to remove to completely disconnect the two black nodes E and I is 2. The cut set is [H, M].

**Final Answer:** Nodes to remove: 2, Cut Set: [H, M].

   - Removing (G, H) ensures that no path through G can reach H.

### Final Conclusion:
Thus, by removing these critical edges, we ensure that there are no paths left from E to H. The minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

**Final Answer**: Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(F, K), (B, N), (G, H)]

   - Path 5: E -> B -> H -> I -> O -> P -> J

   - Paths 3, 4, and 5 share nodes H and I, but they also have unique nodes.

3. **Determine Critical Nodes**:
   - Removing node X will break Path 1.
   - Removing node I will break Paths 2, 3, and 5.
   - Removing node O will break Paths 3 and 4.

4. **Minimal Cut Set**:
   - Removing just node I or node O is not sufficient because it leaves other paths intact.
   - Removing both node I and node X will break all paths between E and J.

Thus, the minimum cut set requires two nodes.

    - Removing node I and F will also ensure disconnection.

   - Removing the edge (H, G) blocks path 4 and parts of path 5.
   - Removing the edge (G, F) blocks remaining parts of path 5 though it effects paths through K too.

6. Removing the edges (H, G), (G, F), and (I, H) results in ensuring no path from H to B.

   - Path 2: N → B → F → E → L → M → J → P → O → C → I → F → X → G → H

   - Path 6: N → C → I → F → X → G → H

   - **Edge (N, B):** Removing this edge breaks Path 1 and cuts off a significant part of the graph from N.
   - **Edge (B, H):** Removing this edge directly breaks Path 1.
   - **Edge (B, F):** Removing this edge breaks several paths that go through B and F.
   - **Edge (I, F):** Removing this edge breaks several paths that go through I and F.

3. **Minimum Cut Set:**
   - Removing just one edge is not sufficient because there are multiple paths.
   - Removing the edges (N, B) and (B, H) breaks Path 1 but leaves other paths intact.
   - Removing the edges (N, B) and (B, F) breaks multiple paths but leaves some paths through C and I intact.
   - Removing the edges (N, B) and (I, F) breaks multiple paths but leaves some paths through C and B intact.
   - Removing the edges (B, H) and (I, F) breaks multiple paths but leaves some paths through N and B intact.

   After careful consideration, the minimum cut set that breaks all paths between N and H is:
   - **Edge (N, B):** Breaks Path 1 and several other paths.
